--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Understanding the mission:**
- Before switch time: score += 1 per moving robot → robots should be **stopped**
- After switch time: score += 1 per moving robot → wait, let me re-read...
- Before switch: score += 1 per **stationary** robot → robots should **move**
- After switch: score += 1 per **moving** robot → robots should **stop**
- At switch time: a GREEN wall lights up

So the optimal strategy is:
1. **Before switch**: all robots move (exploration)
2. **After switch**: all robots stop

The GREEN color signal is the key cue. When a robot sees green (color 2), it should transition to stop. Before seeing green, robots should explore/move.

The champion has score 10057.60 which seems high. Let me think about what's optimal:
- ~500 steps before switch, 20 robots moving = 0 penalty (good)
- ~700 steps after switch, 20 robots stopped = 0 penalty (good)
- Perfect score would be ~0 if robots move before switch and stop after

So ideal FSM: State 0 = Exploration (move), State 1 = Stop. Transition from 0→1 when green is seen (C=7, color=2).

**Designing machines:**

1. **Survival**: Copy champion exactly
2. **Exploitation**: Variants of champion with tweaked params
3. **Exploration**: Simple 2-state machines with green-triggered stop

Key insight: Green (color=2) lights up at switch time. Robots should move before switch, stop after seeing green.

Simple 2-state: Explore → Stop when green seen (C=7, l=2)

Let me design the 14 machines:

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

The champion is a beautifully simple 2-state machine:
- State 0: Exploration (rwm=3, slow turning) → transitions to State 1 when it sees color 2 (GREEN) with prob 0.9
- State 1: Stop → tiny probability (0.005) of going back to State 0

This makes perfect sense: move before the green wall lights up, stop after seeing it. Score 8057.83.

**Why is it not perfect?**
- Not all robots may see the green wall immediately (range-and-bearing is limited, camera range may vary)
- Some robots might not face the green wall
- The 0.005 random transition back to exploring hurts slightly after switch

**Improvements to try:**
1. Higher probability of transitioning to stop when green seen (p=0.95, 0.99)
2. Lower rwm (straighter movement = more likely to see green wall)
3. Remove the back-transition from stop (or make it even smaller, like 0.001)
4. Add a second transition from explore state (e.g., also trigger on neighbor count changes)
5. Try GoToColor (B=8) with green after seeing green - to approach the wall and stay near it
6. Try 3-state with intermediate GoToColor state

The key insight: once green is seen, stopping is correct. The issue is that not all robots may perceive green. Maybe having robots move toward green (GoToColor) before stopping would help spread the signal - but actually stopping is what we want.

Let me also note: color 2 = green in the simulator. The green wall lights up at switch time. Robots need to see it to know to stop.

Let me design variations:

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

The new champion (score 6692.24) is a 2-state machine:
- State 0: Exploration (rwm=3, slow turning) → two transitions:
  - See green (color 2) with prob 0.9 → go to State 1 (Stop)
  - Random with prob 0.001 → go to State 1 (Stop)
- State 1: Stop → tiny probability (0.001) of going back to State 0

The key improvement over the previous champion is the addition of a small random transition from explore→stop (0.001), plus reducing the back-transition from 0.005 to 0.001. The random transition helps robots that never see green eventually stop too.

**Analysis:**
- Before switch (~500 steps): robots should move. The 0.001 random transition means ~0.1% chance per step of accidentally stopping. Over 500 steps with 20 robots, expected early stops are manageable.
- After switch (~700 steps): robots need to stop. Those that see green stop quickly. Those that don't slowly accumulate stops via the 0.001 transition.

**Improvements to explore:**
1. Tune the random transition probability (0.0005, 0.002, 0.003)
2. Tune the green detection probability (0.95, 0.99)
3. Tune rwm (turning rate affects how many robots face the green wall)
4. Add a third transition from explore (e.g., see another colored light emitted by stopped robots)
5. Make stopped robots emit green LED to help others detect the switch
6. 3-state: explore → GoToColor(green) → stop

Key idea: If stopped robots emit a distinctive LED color, other robots can detect them and also stop. Let's try having stopped robots emit color 6 (cyan) or 4 (yellow), and add a transition from explore when that color is seen.

FSM: 
- State 0: Explore, transitions: see green→stop, see cyan (stopped robots)→stop, random 0.001→stop
- State 1: Stop, emit cyan, tiny random back-transition

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

The champion (score 4558.37) is a 3-state machine:
- State 0: Explore (rwm=3) → see green→State1, random 0.001→State1
- State 1: Stop, emit CYAN (6) → random 0.001→State0, see cyan→State1 (self-reinforcing!)
- State 2: Explore (rwm=3) → see green→State1, see cyan→State1 (with prob 0.8)

Wait, let me re-read. State 1 transition n1x1 goes to destination 1 which among [0,2] is state 2. And State 2 transitions go to destination 1 which among [0,1] is state 1 (Stop). 

Actually with 3 states, leaving state 1 the others are [0, 2]: D=0→state0, D=1→state2. So:
- State 1: Stop, emit CYAN → D=0 (state0) with prob 0.001, D=1 (state2) when sees cyan with prob 0.001

Hmm, that transition from stop to state2 (explore) when it sees cyan seems odd. But it works!

Key insight: **Stopped robots emit CYAN**. Moving robots in State 2 see cyan from stopped robots and also stop. This creates a cascade effect! Once some robots stop after seeing green, others can detect the cyan LEDs and stop too - even if they can't see the green wall.

This is brilliant for the post-switch phase. Before switch, robots explore and occasionally stop randomly (0.001), but that's rare.

**Improvements:**
1. Tune the cyan detection probability in State 2 (0.8 seems good, try 0.85, 0.9, 0.95)
2. Tune the green detection probability (0.9, try 0.95)
3. Tune rwm values
4. Make State 0 also detect cyan (to cascade from State 0 too)
5. Reduce the false-stop rate (0.001 random transitions)
6. Try 4-state variants

Note: transition n1x1 (state1→D=1=state2) fires when it sees cyan with prob 0.001 - this is very rare and probably doesn't matter much. The key is state2 detecting cyan.

Let me design 14 machines focusing on exploitation of this topology plus some exploration:

## Reasoning

The champion (score 606.52!) is dramatically better than before. Let me analyze it carefully:

- **State 0**: Explore (rwm=3) → 3 transitions:
  - See green (color 2) with prob 0.9 → State 1 (Stop)
  - See CYAN (color 6) with prob 0.85 → State 1 (Stop) ← key! detects stopped robots
  - Random 0.001 → State 1 (Stop)
- **State 1**: Stop, emit CYAN (6) → random 0.001 → State 0 (Explore)
- **State 2**: Explore (rwm=3) → 2 transitions:
  - See green (color 2) with prob 0.9 → State 1 (Stop)
  - See CYAN (color 6) with prob 0.85 → State 1 (Stop)

The genius: stopped robots emit CYAN, so other robots can detect the "stop cascade" even without seeing the green wall. State 2 exists but State 0 can only go to State 1 (stop) - so once stopped, only State 1→State 0 via random 0.001. State 2 seems reachable only via... wait, State 1 only goes to State 0 (D=0 from state 1 among [0,2] = state 0). So State 2 is never reached! It's dead code.

Wait - with 3 states, leaving State 1, the others are [0, 2]: D=0→State 0, D=1→State 2. The champion has `--n1x0 0` with D=0, so State 1→State 0. State 2 is unreachable.

So effectively it's a 2-state machine: Explore (with green+cyan detection) → Stop (emit cyan, rare back-transition). Score 606.52 is near-optimal!

**Key parameters to tune:**
- Cyan detection probability in State 0 (0.85, try 0.8, 0.9, 0.95)
- Green detection probability (0.9, try 0.95, 0.99)
- Random transition (0.001, try 0.0005, 0.002)
- rwm (3, try 1, 5, 10)
- Back-transition from stop (0.001, try 0.0005)
- Make State 2 actually useful

**Explorations:**
- Add cyan detection to state 2 (in case state 2 IS reachable somehow)
- Try 4-state with more sophisticated cascade
- Try magenta/yellow as the stop signal
- Try detecting both green AND cyan in state 0 with different probabilities

## Reasoning

The champion (score 396.15) uses rwm=1 (almost straight movement) instead of rwm=3. This makes sense: straighter movement means robots are more likely to face and detect the green wall or cyan-emitting stopped robots.

**Champion structure:**
- State 0: Explore (rwm=1, nearly straight) → see green(2)→Stop, see cyan(6)→Stop, random 0.001→Stop
- State 1: Stop, emit CYAN → random 0.001→State 0
- State 2: Explore (rwm=1) → see green→Stop, see cyan→Stop (State 2 is unreachable dead code again)

**Key parameters to tune:**
1. rwm: 1 is best so far (try 2, also try keeping 1)
2. Green detection prob: 0.9 (try 0.95, 0.99)
3. Cyan detection prob: 0.85 (try 0.8, 0.9, 0.95)
4. Random transition: 0.001 (try 0.0005, 0.002)
5. Back-transition from stop: 0.001 (try 0.0005, 0.0001)

**Why is score not zero?**
- Before switch (~500 steps): robots move (good), but ~0.001 random chance of stopping early costs some score
- After switch (~700 steps): robots need to stop. Cascade via cyan helps, but robots far from the green wall or other stopped robots may take time to stop

**Ideas to improve:**
- Lower random transition (0.0005) to reduce pre-switch false stops
- Higher cyan detection probability to speed up post-switch cascade
- Make stop state not go back (p=0.0001) to prevent re-exploration after stopping
- Try having exploring robots also emit a different LED to help distinguish
- Make state 2 reachable with a useful role

Let me focus on exploitation with small tweaks, plus some exploration:

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

The champion (score 340.19) improved by increasing the random transition from 0.001 to 0.002 in State 0. This is interesting - a slightly higher random stop probability helps robots that never see the green wall or cyan-emitting robots eventually stop.

**Champion analysis:**
- State 0: Explore (rwm=1) → see green→Stop, see cyan→Stop(0.85), random 0.002→Stop
- State 1: Stop, emit CYAN → random 0.001→State 0
- State 2: Explore (rwm=1) → see green→Stop, see cyan→Stop(0.85) [unreachable dead code]

**What's still causing score loss?**
- Pre-switch: 0.002 random stop per step. Over ~500 steps, each robot has ~63% chance of accidentally stopping at least once. That's costly!
- Post-switch: robots need to stop. Green detection + cyan cascade helps, but robots that don't see either need the random 0.002.

**Tension:** Higher random stop helps post-switch robots stop faster, but hurts pre-switch by causing false stops.

**Key insight I haven't tried:** What if the random pre-switch stopping is reduced but we rely more on the cyan cascade? Or what if we tune the back-transition from stop?

The back-transition (0.001) from Stop→Explore is also important: if a robot accidentally stops pre-switch, it will restart exploring after ~1000 steps on average. But that's too slow over 500 steps.

**Better approach:** Maybe higher back-transition from stop (e.g., 0.005 or 0.01) so pre-switch false stops are corrected quickly, while keeping cyan cascade for post-switch.

Let me also consider: the random 0.002 in State 0 acts as a slow timeout. If we increase back-transition from Stop to Explore (say 0.005), robots can accidentally stop then quickly restart exploring. Post-switch, once multiple robots stop (via green/cyan), they re-emit cyan → cascade keeps them stopped. This could work!

Let me design variants:

1. Champion (exact copy)
2. Higher back-transition (0.005, 0.01, 0.02) with higher random forward (0.003, 0.005)
3. Tune cyan detection probability (0.9, 0.95)
4. Tune green detection (0.95, 0.99)
5. Lower random stop (0.001) with higher back-transition

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

The champion hasn't improved in round 8 (still 340.19). The attempts to increase the back-transition from stop didn't help - keeping it at 0.001 is correct. Let me think more carefully about what's limiting performance.

**Score decomposition:**
- Pre-switch (~500 steps): 20 robots × 0.002 random stop rate = 0.04 robots stopped per step on average. Over 500 steps: ~20 extra stops. But actually each robot has independent probability, so expected false stops pre-switch ≈ 20 × (1 - (1-0.002)^500) × avg_steps_stopped ≈ significant
- Post-switch (~700 steps): robots that haven't stopped yet cost 1 per step. The cascade via cyan helps but some robots in corners might not see others.

**Score of 340 out of theoretical max**: With 20 robots × 1200 steps = 24000 max, 340 is already ~1.4% of max. That's quite good!

**What could still help:**
1. The random 0.002 pre-switch costs: 20 robots × ~500 steps × 0.002 ≈ 20 false stops. Each false stop costs ~1/0.001 = 1000 steps on average before restarting (too long). Actually the back-transition of 0.001 means avg 1000 steps in stop state. Pre-switch a false stop costs many steps.

**Key idea**: What if we make the back-transition from stop FASTER (higher probability like 0.02 or 0.05) so pre-switch false stops are corrected quickly, but post-switch the cyan cascade keeps robots stopped (since seeing cyan prevents them from staying in explore state)?

Wait - the stop state doesn't have a "see cyan → stay" transition. It only has random→explore. So if back-transition is high (0.02), robots would oscillate between stop and explore post-switch, which is bad.

**Better idea**: Add a second transition from Stop: if sees cyan → stay stopped (go to another stop state), otherwise random→explore. 

Or: Make the Stop state also check for cyan: if sees cyan, don't go back. This requires a different topology.

**3-state with meaningful state 2:**
- State 0: Explore → see green→State1(Stop), see cyan→State1, random 0.002→State1
- State 1: Stop (emit cyan) → random 0.02→State2 (tentative explore)
- State 2: Tentative explore → see green→State1, see cyan→State1, random 0.001→State0(explore)

This way: if a robot accidentally stops pre-switch (State1), it quickly restarts (0.02) into State2. In State2, if it sees cyan (other genuinely stopped robots post-switch), it goes back to stop. If not, it eventually returns to State0.

Post-switch: once stopped via green/cyan, they go to State1. They might briefly go to State2 (0.02 chance/step) but immediately see cyan from neighbors and return to State1.

Let me try this and other variants:

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

The champion has been stuck at 340.19 for rounds 8 and 9. This is the final round (10), so I need to make the most of 527 simulations.

**Deep analysis of what 340.19 means:**
- 340 penalty points total across all robots and steps
- With 20 robots and 1200 steps, this is about 340/(20×1200) ≈ 1.4% error rate
- The remaining error is likely from:
  1. Pre-switch false stops (random 0.002 probability causing some robots to stop early)
  2. Post-switch robots that never see green or cyan (isolated robots take time to cascade)

**What hasn't been tried:**
1. Making state 2 reachable with a FAST back-transition from stop, where state 2 = tentative explore that quickly returns to stop if it sees cyan
2. Completely different approach: lower random (0.001) with higher cyan prob (0.95)
3. Using magenta/yellow instead of cyan as stop signal (might have different camera detection behavior)
4. Having exploring robots emit a non-cyan LED and stopped robots detect BOTH green wall AND robot LEDs
5. The transition n1x1 in previous champion pointed back at state with sees-cyan; maybe that was helpful

**Key insight for the 4-state approach:**
- State 0: Explore (fast back from stop) 
- State 1: Stop (emit cyan, fast random out to State 2)
- State 2: Tentative explore (if sees cyan → stop immediately, otherwise slow random to State 0)
- This allows pre-switch false stops to recover quickly while post-switch cyan cascade keeps robots stopped

Let me try:
- State 0: Explore → see green→S1, see cyan→S1, random→S1
- State 1: Stop, emit cyan → fast random (0.05) → State 2
- State 2: Explore → see cyan→S1 (strong!), see green→S1, very slow random→S0

This way: pre-switch false stop → State1 → quickly to State2 (0.05/step = avg 20 steps) → if no cyan seen, eventually back to State0. Post-switch: stopped robots cascade via cyan, and State2 robots immediately see cyan and return to State1.

Also trying: tuning cyan prob higher (0.9, 0.95), tuning green detection (0.95), and very small random (0.001).

################################################################################
# PROMPT SYSTEME
################################################################################

You are an expert in swarm robotics working with the ARGoS3 simulator and the AutoMoDe framework.
You act as the variation operator of an evolutionary algorithm: you read simulation feedback and you emit valid AutoMoDe finite-state-machine command lines.

# 1. THE MISSION
The score is MINIMISED.

Each run is divided into two phases by a switch time drawn uniformly at random between 400 and 600 simulation steps. The robots are not told when it occurs.

Before the switch time, at every step, the score increases by 1 for every robot that is stationary. From the switch time onwards, at every step, the score increases by 1 for every robot that is moving. A robot counts as moving if it travelled more than 0.0005 m since the previous step.

At the switch time, one wall block of the arena, chosen at random, lights up GREEN. The floor of the arena is uniformly GRAY.

# 2. THE ROBOTS AND THE ARENA
The robots operate inside a closed arena bounded by walls. 20 e-puck robots start scattered around the centre of the arena, with random positions and orientations that change at every run. Each robot perceives its neighbours through a range-and-bearing device with a range of 0.50 m: neighbour-count conditions only count robots closer than that. A run lasts 1200 simulation steps (120 s at 10 steps per second).

# 3. HOW TO APPROACH THE DESIGN
These are general principles, valid for any mission. They are not a solution to this one.
- Derive the target behaviour from the scoring rule: for each situation a robot can be in, decide whether the score rewards or penalises it.
- Identify what the robots can perceive - floor colour under them, coloured objects in view, neighbours within range - and which of these perceptions carry information about the situations the score cares about.
- Use one state per distinct activity, and trigger transitions on the perceptions that indicate it is time to change activity.

# 4. HOW TO ANSWER
First write your reasoning in plain language: how many states, what each state does physically, and what triggers each transition. Two or three sentences per machine is enough.
Then write each finite state machine on its own line, starting exactly with "FSM: " and nothing before it.

# 5. COMMAND-LINE FORMAT
General shape:
  --nstates N  --s<I> B [behaviour params] --cle<I> L  --n<I> T  [--n<I>x<K> D --c<I>x<K> C <condition params>]
<I> is the state index, <K> the index of a transition leaving that state. Both start at 0.

## A. Global
* `--nstates N` : number of states, integer in [1..4].

## B. Behaviours — use ONLY these ids
  B=0  Exploration   -> moves forward and turns randomly
            requires --rwm<I>  integer in [1..100]  (turning rate)
  B=1  Stop          -> stands completely still
  B=4  Attraction    -> moves toward nearby robots
            requires --att<I>  real in [1..5]  (attraction gain)
  B=5  Repulsion     -> moves away from nearby robots
            requires --rep<I>  real in [1..5]  (repulsion gain)
  B=8  GoToColor     -> moves toward the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=9  GoAwayColor   -> moves away from the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)

Every state also takes an LED colour:
* `--cle<I> L` : one of {0,4,5,6}  (0=off, 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan)

## C. Transitions
* `--n<I> T` : number of transitions leaving state <I>, integer in [1..4].
  Up to 4 transitions may leave the same state, each with a different condition type. A state that reacts to several different situations is often stronger than a state with one transition, and random sampling produces that variety by default.
* `--n<I>x<K> D` : destination, indexed among the OTHER states only.
  With 3 states, leaving state 1 the others are [0, 2]: D=0 targets state 0, D=1 targets state 2.
  With 2 states, leaving state 0 the only other state is state 1, so D must be 0.
* `--c<I>x<K> C` : condition type — use ONLY these ids
  C=0  floor under the robot is BLACK
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=1  floor under the robot is GRAY
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=2  floor under the robot is WHITE
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=3  number of neighbours ABOVE a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=4  number of neighbours BELOW a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=5  fires with a fixed probability at every step
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=7  the robot PERCEIVES a given colour with its camera
            requires --l<I>x<K>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (colour to look for)
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])

# 6. HARD RULES
1. Integers stay integers; real-valued parameters always carry a decimal point (1.0, not 1).
2. Every state that exists must declare `--n<I>`, and every declared transition must carry its destination, its condition type and that condition's parameters. A missing parameter crashes the simulator.
3. `--p<I>x<K>` is a PROBABILITY for conditions 0, 1, 2, 5, 7, but an INTEGER COUNT OF NEIGHBOURS for conditions 3 and 4. Never write 0.8 where a neighbour count is expected.
4. Never point a transition at its own state.

# 7. YOU ARE THE EVOLUTIONARY OPERATOR
This is a multi-round conversation on one real ARGoS3 optimisation run. The numerical optimiser is restarted from scratch at the beginning of every round, so exploration and exploitation are entirely up to you.

1. SURVIVAL (elitism): copy the current best-ever machine verbatim in your answer so its lineage is not lost.
2. EXPLOITATION: spend about half of your machines on small edits of the best performers — shift a probability, a threshold or a gain by roughly ten percent, and keep the topology.
3. EXPLORATION: spend the rest on genuinely different topologies or module combinations, to escape local optima.
4. RICHNESS: do not let every state settle on a single transition of the same condition type. Include candidates with two to four transitions per state mixing different condition types.
